# Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees

Zihe Gao  Zhengling Qi  Yifan Cui    Zhejiang University    George
Washington University

###### Abstract

Recursive self-improvement offers a promising path toward AI agents that
improve through experience by refining their own harnesses without
retraining their underlying large language models (LLMs). Making this
self-improvement process reliable, however, requires more than
generating new updates: agents must decide which changes are worth
keeping. A greedy gate that accepts updates based solely on higher
validation scores can mistake an observed gain for genuine progress and
lead to performance degeneration, whereas overly conservative
statistical gates may reject beneficial updates and hinder performance
improvement. We introduce Trustworthy Recursive Updates with Statistical
Testing (TRUST), a framework that adapts an online multiple hypothesis
testing procedure to control the rate of false evolution across rounds,
with an LLM analyzing historical context to help determine how much
evidence is required to update the incumbent. Under interpretable
assumptions, we prove that TRUST limits the proportion of accepted
updates that result in unacceptable performance loss. We further derive
a lower bound on the final performance gain. Across the benchmarks
HotpotQA, DROP, and APPS with the models DeepSeek-V4-Flash and
Qwen-3.8-Flash, TRUST reduces the average ratio of accepted inferior
updates from the greedy gate’s 45.59% to 2.78%, while achieving higher
final scores than the state-of-the-art statistical gate baselines for
recursive self-improvement. These findings demonstrate that TRUST
strikes a balance between statistical reliability and performance gain
in recursive self-improvement.

|     |
| --- |
|     |

## 1 Introduction

Recursive self-improvement (RSI) has attracted growing interest for its
potential to enhance agents’ capabilities without retraining their
underlying language models ([Ren et al., 2026](#bib.bib22); [Chen et
al., 2026](#bib.bib4)). In a self-improvement loop, agents modify their
own prompts, memory, tools, and workflows to improve task performance
([Madaan et al., 2023](#bib.bib18); [Shinn et al., 2023](#bib.bib31);
[Wang et al., 2024](#bib.bib34)). This loop can be organized into three
components: a solver that executes tasks, a proposer that generates
candidate updates, and a gate that decides whether to accept them ([Wang
et al., 2026](#bib.bib35); [Ren et al., 2026](#bib.bib22)).

Figure 1: Gain trajectories of TRUST and Greedy on HotpotQA with
DeepSeek-V4-Flash. Details are in
Section [6.1](#S6.SS1 "6.1 Main gate comparison ‣ 6 Numerical Experiments ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees").

Figure 2: Recursive self-improvement with our trustworthy gate TRUST.
TRUST combines noninferiority, overlap correction, e-value testing and
error budget allocation with FER control across rounds.

Existing work has explored mechanisms for generating better candidate
updates, while less attention has been paid to deciding when to accept
them. A common acceptance approach is to use a greedy gate, which
accepts a candidate whenever its score on a fixed validation set exceeds
the incumbent’s ([Shawn, 2026](#bib.bib41)).
Figure [1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
illustrates how early gain under greedy acceptance can be lost in
subsequent rounds. Noise from finite samples and overfitting from
reusing validation data can both make inferior updates appear beneficial
and pass a greedy gate ([Dwork et al., 2015](#bib.bib8); [Rogers et al.,
2016](#bib.bib23)). Accepting a harmful update can reduce the agent’s
current performance. The resulting loss may persist through subsequent
rounds, ultimately limiting the gain for self-improvement.

Recent work has begun to focus on the acceptance gate ([Wu et al.,
2025](#bib.bib37); [Shawn, 2026](#bib.bib41); [Sengupta,
2026](#bib.bib25)). RSEA uses an extra validation set separate from the
proposal generation set to select candidate updates ([Nguyen et al.,
2026](#bib.bib19)). PACE provides a safety guarantee for each candidate
update through an e-process test in a single round instead of the entire
recursive self-improvement process ([Shawn, 2026](#bib.bib41)). However,
controlling the probability of any false acceptance across rounds can
make statistical gates conservative and hinder beneficial updates ([Wu
et al., 2025](#bib.bib37); [Sengupta, 2026](#bib.bib25)). A key
challenge, therefore, remains: retaining opportunities for useful
improvement while controlling acceptance errors across rounds under
limited validation resources.

We introduce Trustworthy Recursive Updates with Statistical Testing
(TRUST), a general statistical acceptance framework. Motivated by false
discovery rate (FDR) control ([Benjamini and Hochberg, 1995](#bib.bib2);
[Xu and Ramdas, 2024](#bib.bib38); [Zhang et al., 2025b](#bib.bib43)),
we define the false evolution rate (FER) as a safety criterion for
recursive self-improvement. FER measures the expected proportion of
accepted updates that cause an unacceptable performance loss, thereby
quantifying the gate’s false acceptance risk. To control FER, we
formulate the sequential acceptance decisions as an online multiple
testing problem. We also use an LLM to analyze historical context and
help the error budget allocator assign less restrictive testing levels
to promising candidates. We further draw on noninferiority testing to
improve statistical power by allowing a possible loss tolerance
([Blackwelder, 1982](#bib.bib3); [Da Silva et al., 2009](#bib.bib32);
[Schumi and Wittes, 2011](#bib.bib24)), and use overlap correction to
account for bias from reusing validation samples across rounds ([Dwork
et al., 2015](#bib.bib8); [Rogers et al., 2016](#bib.bib23)).
Figure [2](#S1.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
summarizes how TRUST makes recursive self-improvement trustworthy.

Our contributions. We make the following three main contributions: (a)
Our framework builds on online multiple hypothesis testing with
interpretable parameters, including the error budget, loss tolerance,
and overlap correction strength. It naturally incorporates LLM guidance
based on diverse forms of historical context. (b) We establish
statistical guarantees under explicit assumptions, including FER
control, power consistency for improving candidates, and a lower bound
on deployment gain. These results connect hypothesis testing criteria to
performance gain in recursive self-improvement. (c) We evaluate TRUST in
a closed-loop self-improvement harness across HotpotQA, DROP, and APPS
with DeepSeek-V4-Flash and Qwen-3.8-Flash. Averaged across these
settings, TRUST achieves the highest final gain of 24.84 pp. Compared
with the greedy gate, TRUST reduces the mean FER₀ from 45.59% to 2.78%
and the mean maximum drawdown from 4.963 pp to 0.047 pp, demonstrating
high performance gains with few erroneous acceptances and small
drawdowns.

## 2 Related Work

#### Recursive self-improvement and agent harness.

An agent harness includes tools, context management, and execution
control ([Shi et al., 2026](#bib.bib51)). Recursive self-improvement
agents use feedback to improve various harness components. Promptbreeder
evolves task prompts ([Fernando et al., 2024](#bib.bib9)). Reflexion,
ExpeL, and Voyager convert experience into reflections, insights, and
reusable skills, respectively ([Shinn et al., 2023](#bib.bib31); [Zhao
et al., 2024](#bib.bib44); [Wang et al., 2024](#bib.bib34)). CoEvo-Mem
improves memory retrieval ([Ye et al., 2026](#bib.bib40)). AFlow and
Flow refine agentic workflows ([Zhang et al., 2025a](#bib.bib42); [Niu
et al., 2025](#bib.bib52)). AgentSquare defines a modular design space
with planning, reasoning, tool use, and memory components ([Shang et
al., 2025](#bib.bib29)). These works focus on proposing effective
candidates, while we study whether a new candidate should be accepted to
make recursive self-improvement trustworthy. Additionally, we design a
harness with diverse editable components in our experiments to evaluate
how different gates affect performance and risk.

#### Hypothesis testing methods.

Multiple hypothesis testing procedures are primarily based on two error
criteria: the familywise error rate (FWER) and the FDR ([Benjamini and
Hochberg, 1995](#bib.bib2); [Wang and Ramdas, 2022](#bib.bib49);
[Robertson et al., 2023](#bib.bib48)). FWER control is generally more
conservative than FDR control. Many popular online FDR methods with
p-values rely on independence across tests ([Ramdas et al.,
2018](#bib.bib50); [Robertson et al., 2023](#bib.bib48)). This
independence can be difficult to satisfy when agents evolve based on
past feedback. The e-value is an alternative test statistic with a
betting interpretation ([Shafer, 2021](#bib.bib27)). The e-BH and e-LOND
procedures allow arbitrary dependence among valid e-values while
controlling FDR ([Wang and Ramdas, 2022](#bib.bib49); [Xu and Ramdas,
2024](#bib.bib38)). Drawing on e-GAI ([Zhang et al., 2025b](#bib.bib43))
and contextual online testing ([Chen and Kasiviswanathan,
2020](#bib.bib6)), TRUST constructs an e-value and allocates the testing
levels with context. In RSI, SGM uses statistical tests to decide
updates to control FWER across rounds ([Wu et al., 2025](#bib.bib37)),
while PACE uses an e-process to control the probability of false
acceptance in a single round ([Shawn, 2026](#bib.bib41)). TRUST provides
FER control across rounds and achieves higher final scores than these
statistical baselines in our experiments.

## 3 Problem Formulation

Let $`\mathcal{D}`$ denote the distribution of tasks and let
$`u(x,s)\in[0,1]`$ denote the score of output $`s`$ on task $`x`$. For a
solver $`\theta`$, let $`S_{\theta}(x,\Omega)`$ denote the solver output
on task $`x`$ under execution randomness $`\Omega`$. Define the
population score of $`\theta`$ as

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
J(\theta)=\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{D},\Omega}\left[u\bigl(X,S_{\theta}(X,\Omega)\bigr)\right].
``` |  | (1) |

We consider a self-improvement process that starts with an initial
incumbent solver $`\Theta_{0}`$ and initial information
$`\mathcal{F}_{0}`$. It recursively generates and evaluates candidates
over $`T`$ rounds. In round $`t`$, let $`Q(\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1})`$
denote the conditional distribution of the candidate given the completed
history $`\mathcal{F}_{t-1}`$ from the fixed proposer. The proposer
generates
$`\widetilde{\Theta}_{t}\sim Q\!\left(\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right)`$,
which is evaluated by the gate $`\mathcal{G}`$ against the previous
incumbent $`\Theta_{t-1}`$. Then the gate $`\mathcal{G}`$ produces
$`A_{t}\in\{0,1\}`$ and updates the incumbent according to

|     |                                                            |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------------|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \Theta_{t}=\begin{cases}\widetilde{\Theta}_{t},&A_{t}=1,\\
       \Theta_{t-1},&A_{t}=0.\end{cases}
       ```                                                         |     |

Different gates may induce different acceptance decisions, thereby
changing the subsequent self-improvement trajectories and the candidates
proposed in later rounds. These changes can affect the final gain, which
is defined as

|     |                                                            |     |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       G(T)=\mathbb{E}\!\left[J(\Theta_{T})-J(\Theta_{0})\right].
       ```                                                         |     | (2) |

We next define the core safety criterion. The population score
difference between the selected candidate and the incumbent is
$`\Delta_{t}=J\!\left(\widetilde{\Theta}_{t}\right)-J\!\left(\Theta_{t-1}\right).`$
For a given tolerance level $`\varepsilon\in[0,1)`$, let
$`N_{t}^{\varepsilon}=\mathbf{1}\{\Delta_{t}\leq-\varepsilon\}`$,
$`R_{T}=\sum_{t\leq T}A_{t}`$, and
$`F_{T}^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{t\leq T}N_{t}^{\varepsilon}A_{t}`$. Define
the false evolution rate (FER) at tolerance $`\varepsilon`$ as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\operatorname{FER}_{\varepsilon}(T)=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{F_{T}^{\varepsilon}}{R_{T}\vee 1}\right].
``` |  | (3) |

Here, FER is the expected proportion of unacceptably inferior updates
among accepted updates, and $`\varepsilon`$ specifies the tolerated
loss. For $`\varepsilon>0`$, the difference $`\Delta_{t}`$ is divided
into three regions: updates with $`\Delta_{t}\leq-\varepsilon`$ are
unacceptable, those with $`-\varepsilon<\Delta_{t}\leq 0`$ are tolerated
but not improving, and those with $`\Delta_{t}>0`$ are improving. Our
goal is to ensure that $`\operatorname{FER}_{\varepsilon}(T)\leq q`$ for
a prespecified FER budget $`q`$, thereby providing a statistical safety
guarantee for the entire self-improvement process. This helps avoid
unacceptable updates, retain beneficial proposals, and may achieve a
favorable final gain.

Let $`\mathcal{V}_{N}=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{N})\sim\mathcal{D}^{N}`$ be a
fixed validation pool of size $`N`$. Candidate generation uses
information from the completed history. In round $`t`$, the sampling
plan is to draw $`n\leq N`$ tasks uniformly without replacement from
$`\mathcal{V}_{N}`$ to evaluate the current candidate. All sampled tasks
are then returned to $`\mathcal{V}_{N}`$ so that the next round starts
with the full validation pool. Let $`K_{t,i}`$ denote the pool index
selected at the $`i`$th validation draw, and let $`X_{t,i}=Z_{K_{t,i}}`$
be the corresponding task. On task $`X_{t,i}`$, the candidate and
incumbent produce the observed score difference

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
D_{t,i}={}u\bigl(X_{t,i},S_{\widetilde{\Theta}_{t}}(X_{t,i},\Omega^{c}_{t,i})\bigr)-u\bigl(X_{t,i},S_{\Theta_{t-1}}(X_{t,i},\Omega^{0}_{t,i})\bigr)\in[-1,1],
``` |  | (4) |

where $`\Omega^{c}_{t,i}`$ and $`\Omega^{0}_{t,i}`$ denote the execution
randomness of the candidate and incumbent, respectively.

#### Information flow.

Our framework uses filtrations to describe how information accumulates
within and across rounds ([Ramdas et al., 2023](#bib.bib21)). For
clarity, we briefly explain this information flow. The initial
information $`\mathcal{F}_{0}`$ is independent of the validation pool.
Let $`\mathcal{F}_{t-1}`$ denote the completed history before round
$`t`$. Before validation, $`\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{pre}}_{t}`$
additionally fixes the selected candidate $`\widetilde{\Theta}_{t}`$ and
testing level $`\alpha_{t}`$ in
Section [4](#S4 "4 Trustworthy Recursive Updates with Statistical Testing ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees").
For $`i=1,\ldots,n`$, let $`\mathcal{H}_{t,i}`$ augment
$`\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{pre}}_{t}`$ with the first $`i`$ validation
samples and their paired outcomes, with
$`\mathcal{H}_{t,0}=\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{pre}}_{t}`$. After validation,
$`\mathcal{F}_{t}`$ contains the decision $`A_{t}`$ and updated
incumbent $`\Theta_{t}`$. We illustrate this timeline in
Figure [3](#S3.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ Information flow. ‣ 3 Problem Formulation ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees").

Figure 3: Information flow in a self-improvement round:
$`\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{pre}}_{t}=\mathcal{H}_{t,0}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{t,1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{t,n}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{t}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{pre}}_{t+1}`$.
The relation $`\subseteq`$ denotes inclusion of information.

## 4 Trustworthy Recursive Updates with Statistical Testing

The greedy gate accepts a candidate whenever the empirical score
difference exceeds zero, making the acceptance decision sensitive to
sampling and execution randomness. We formulate candidate acceptance as
a hypothesis testing problem. In round $`t`$, we test

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
H_{t}^{0}:\Delta_{t}\leq-\varepsilon,\qquad H_{t}^{1}:\Delta_{t}>-\varepsilon.
``` |  | (5) |

If the null hypothesis $`H_{t}^{0}`$ is rejected, the selected candidate
is accepted as the new incumbent. Otherwise, the previous incumbent is
retained.

In online FDR control, testing levels are allocated across multiple
hypotheses under an overall error budget ([Chen and Kasiviswanathan,
2020](#bib.bib6); [Zhang et al., 2025b](#bib.bib43)). TRUST adapts this
framework to the sequence of candidate acceptance tests defined in
([5](#S4.E5 "In 4 Trustworthy Recursive Updates with Statistical Testing ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")),
using $`q`$ as the total FER budget. In round $`t`$, the testing level
$`\alpha_{t}`$ determines the acceptance threshold, while the test
statistic $`M_{t,m}^{(\beta)}`$ measures the evidence accumulated from
the first $`m`$ validation observations. For $`\alpha_{t}>0`$, TRUST
accepts the candidate at the first $`m\leq n`$ when
$`M_{t,m}^{(\beta)}\geq 1/\alpha_{t}`$, following the standard testing
rule for sequential e-value testing ([Ramdas et al., 2023](#bib.bib21)).
If the threshold is not reached within $`n`$ observations, the incumbent
is retained. When $`\alpha_{t}=0`$, validation is skipped and the
incumbent is retained. We next describe how $`\alpha_{t}`$ is allocated
and how $`M_{t,m}^{(\beta)}`$ is constructed.

### 4.1 Testing Level Allocation with Context

We now describe how TRUST allocates the testing level $`\alpha_{t}`$ in
round $`t`$ under the total FER budget $`q`$. Let $`B_{t}`$ denote the
remaining FER budget before round $`t`$, with $`B_{1}=q\in(0,1)`$, and
let $`\rho_{t}\in(0,1)`$ denote the spending fraction of the remaining
FER budget in round $`t`$.

Adapting the e-LORD allocation rule within the e-GAI framework ([Zhang
et al., 2025b](#bib.bib43)), we combine the adjusted spending fraction
$`\rho_{t}`$ with the remaining budget and the number of accepted
updates to determine the testing level

|     |                                                            |     |     |
|-----|------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \alpha_{t}=\min\{\alpha_{\max},\rho_{t}B_{t}(R_{t-1}+1)\},
       ```                                                         |     | (6) |

where $`\alpha_{\max}\in(0,1)`$. The resulting charge $`c_{t}`$ for the
FER budget and the remaining budget $`B_{t+1}`$ are

|     |                                                                |     |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------------------------|-----|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       c_{t}=\frac{\alpha_{t}}{R_{t-1}+1},\qquad B_{t+1}=B_{t}-c_{t}.
       ```                                                             |     | (7) |

The charge satisfies $`0\leq c_{t}\leq\rho_{t}B_{t}\leq B_{t}`$, keeping
the balance nonnegative.

We next describe how to determine $`\rho_{t}`$. Building on the
contextual error budget allocation in online multiple hypothesis testing
([Chen and Kasiviswanathan, 2020](#bib.bib6)), we make use of the
context $`C_{t}`$ in round $`t`$ and use an LLM to analyze it. This
context only contains information available before the current round’s
validation, including the candidate update and feedback from previous
validation rounds. Following the evaluation structure of G-Eval ([Liu et
al., 2023](#bib.bib16)), the LLM uses explicit criteria to assess the
candidate’s potential benefits and risks. Let $`\Psi`$ denote the
mapping from context to LLM multipliers, with
$`w_{t}=\Psi(C_{t})\in[0,\infty)`$. The default spending fraction is set
to $`\rho_{t}^{0}=1/(T-t+1)`$, corresponding to an equal share of the
remaining FER budget. The LLM multiplier adjusts the spending fraction
within the range $`[\rho_{\min},\rho_{\max}]`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\rho_{t}=\operatorname{clip}(w_{t}\rho_{t}^{0},\rho_{\min},\rho_{\max}),
``` |  | (8) |

where $`0<\rho_{\min}<\rho_{\max}<1`$ and
$`\operatorname{clip}(x,a,b)=\min\{b,\max\{a,x\}\}`$. All allocation
quantities are determined before validation and included in the
information $`\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{pre}}_{t}`$ in
Figure [3](#S3.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ Information flow. ‣ 3 Problem Formulation ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees").

### 4.2 E-Value Construction with Overlap Correction

Once the testing level $`\alpha_{t}`$ has been determined, the next step
is to construct the test statistic $`M_{t,m}^{(\beta)}`$. We first
correct for bias from reusing validation samples. Based on the sampling
procedure in
Section [3](#S3 "3 Problem Formulation ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees"),
we define the following index sets to characterize overlap across
validation rounds. Recall that $`K_{t,i}`$ denotes the index of the
$`i`$th validation sample in round $`t`$. Let $`\mathcal{R}_{t,i-1}`$
denote the set of indices not sampled in the first $`i-1`$ draws of
round $`t`$, and let $`\mathcal{I}_{t,i-1}`$ denote the cumulative set
of indices used in validation across the first $`t-1`$ rounds and the
first $`i-1`$ draws of round $`t`$. For each draw, define

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
Q_{t,i}=\mathbf{1}\{K_{t,i}\in\mathcal{I}_{t,i-1}\},\qquad\zeta_{t,i}=\mathbb{P}(Q_{t,i}=1\mid\mathcal{H}_{t,i-1})=\frac{|\mathcal{I}_{t,i-1}\cap\mathcal{R}_{t,i-1}|}{|\mathcal{R}_{t,i-1}|}.
``` |  | (9) |

Here, $`Q_{t,i}`$ indicates whether the current sample has previously
been used in validation, while $`\zeta_{t,i}`$ represents its
probability under uniform sampling.

0:  Number of rounds $`T`$, validation pool $`\mathcal{V}_{N}`$, sample
size $`1\leq n\leq N`$, initial incumbent $`\Theta_{0}`$, proposer
$`Q`$, allocator $`\Psi`$, budget $`q`$, margin $`\varepsilon`$,
strength $`\beta`$, $`\alpha_{\max}`$, $`0<\rho_{\min}<\rho_{\max}<1`$.

1:  Initialize $`B_{1}\leftarrow q`$, $`R_{0}\leftarrow 0`$, and
$`\mathcal{I}_{1,0}\leftarrow\varnothing`$.

2:  for $`t=1,\ldots,T`$ do

3:    Initialize $`\tau_{t}\leftarrow n`$, $`A_{t}\leftarrow 0`$,
$`\mathcal{R}_{t,0}\leftarrow\{1,\ldots,N\}`$, and
$`M^{(\beta)}_{t,0}\leftarrow 1`$.

4:    Propose
$`\widetilde{\Theta}_{t}\sim Q(\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1})`$ and
construct context $`C_{t}`$.

5:    Set $`w_{t}\leftarrow\Psi(C_{t})`$ and
$`\rho_{t}\leftarrow\operatorname{clip}\bigl(w_{t}/(T-t+1),\rho_{\min},\rho_{\max}\bigr)`$.

6:    Allocate
$`\alpha_{t}\leftarrow\min\{\alpha_{\max},\rho_{t}B_{t}(R_{t-1}+1)\}`$.

7:    Update $`B_{t+1}\leftarrow B_{t}-\alpha_{t}/(R_{t-1}+1)`$.

8:    for $`i=1,\ldots,n`$ do

9:     Draw $`K_{t,i}`$ uniformly from $`\mathcal{R}_{t,i-1}`$ and
evaluate $`D_{t,i}`$ on $`X_{t,i}=Z_{K_{t,i}}`$
by ([4](#S3.E4 "In 3 Problem Formulation ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")).

10:     Compute $`M^{(\beta)}_{t,i}`$ using $`D_{t,i}`$,
$`\mathcal{R}_{t,i-1}`$ and $`\mathcal{I}_{t,i-1}`$
by ([9](#S4.E9 "In 4.2 E-Value Construction with Overlap Correction ‣ 4 Trustworthy Recursive Updates with Statistical Testing ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees"))
and ([10](#S4.E10 "In 4.2 E-Value Construction with Overlap Correction ‣ 4 Trustworthy Recursive Updates with Statistical Testing ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")).

11:     Update
$`\mathcal{R}_{t,i}\leftarrow\mathcal{R}_{t,i-1}\setminus\{K_{t,i}\}`$
and $`\mathcal{I}_{t,i}\leftarrow\mathcal{I}_{t,i-1}\cup\{K_{t,i}\}`$.

12:     if $`M^{(\beta)}_{t,i}\geq 1/\alpha_{t}`$ then
$`A_{t}\leftarrow 1`$, $`\tau_{t}\leftarrow i`$, break.

13:    end for

14:    Update $`\mathcal{I}_{t+1,0}\leftarrow\mathcal{I}_{t,\tau_{t}}`$.

15:    Set $`\Theta_{t}\leftarrow\widetilde{\Theta}_{t}`$ if
$`A_{t}=1`$, and $`\Theta_{t}\leftarrow\Theta_{t-1}`$ otherwise.

16:    Update $`R_{t}\leftarrow R_{t-1}+A_{t}`$.

17:  end for

18:  return $`\Theta_{T}`$.

Algorithm 1 TRUST

We rescale the score difference in
([4](#S3.E4 "In 3 Problem Formulation ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees"))
from $`[-1,1]`$ to $`[0,1]`$ by setting $`Y_{t,i}=(1+D_{t,i})/2`$, which
allows us to use standard e-value constructions for bounded observations
([Waudby-Smith and Ramdas, 2024](#bib.bib36); [Ramdas et al.,
2023](#bib.bib21)), and the corresponding null boundary in
([5](#S4.E5 "In 4 Trustworthy Recursive Updates with Statistical Testing ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees"))
becomes $`p_{\varepsilon}=(1-\varepsilon)/2`$. We choose
$`\beta\in[0,1-p_{\varepsilon}]`$ as the correction strength for bias
from data reuse and weight this strength by the exposure probability
$`\zeta_{t,i}`$ to obtain the corrected null boundary
$`u_{t,i}^{(\beta)}=p_{\varepsilon}+\zeta_{t,i}\beta.`$ The sequential
e-value evidence is accumulated by setting $`M_{t,0}^{(\beta)}=1`$ and

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
M_{t,m}^{(\beta)}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\left[1+\eta_{t,i}\frac{Y_{t,i}-u_{t,i}^{(\beta)}}{u_{t,i}^{(\beta)}}\right],\qquad m=1,\ldots,n.
``` |  | (10) |

The betting fraction $`\eta_{t,i}\in[0,1)`$ is chosen based on the
information in $`\mathcal{H}_{t,i-1}`$ before observing $`Y_{t,i}`$ and
controls how strongly the current observation changes the evidence. It
can be selected using standard adaptive betting strategies
([Waudby-Smith and Ramdas, 2024](#bib.bib36); [Orabona and Jun,
2024](#bib.bib20); [Shekhar and Ramdas, 2023](#bib.bib26)). The complete
procedure is presented in
Algorithm [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ 4.2 E-Value Construction with Overlap Correction ‣ 4 Trustworthy Recursive Updates with Statistical Testing ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees").

## 5 Theoretical Analysis

We now establish statistical guarantees of our framework, including the
validity of the e-value, FER control, power consistency to accept
improvements, and a lower bound on the final gain.

###### Assumption 1.

For any $`t=1,\ldots,T`$ and $`i=1,\ldots,n`$, suppose

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}[Y_{t,i}\mid\mathcal{H}_{t,i-1},Q_{t,i}=1]\leq p_{\varepsilon}+\beta,\quad\text{if }H_{t}^{0}\text{ is true and }\zeta_{t,i}>0.
``` |  | (11) |

Reusing validation feedback to construct or select later candidates can
lead to optimistic bias in their estimated performance, which is widely
studied in adaptive data analysis ([Dwork et al., 2015](#bib.bib8);
[Rogers et al., 2016](#bib.bib23)). We use $`\beta`$ to represent the
strength of the correction for data reuse.
Assumption [1](#Thmassumption1 "Assumption 1. ‣ 5 Theoretical Analysis ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
connects this correction to our testing by bounding the conditional mean
in the e-value construction. We discuss the relationship between
$`\beta`$ and our algorithm in detail in
Appendix [A.1](#A1.SS1 "A.1 Interpretation of the overlap correction ‣ Appendix A Supplementary Discussion ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees").

TRUST may accept the candidate early when the testing threshold is
reached and the number of observations is a random variable determined
by the observed evidence rather than a fixed number. We formalize this
as a stopping time ([Durrett, 2019](#bib.bib47)). A random variable
$`\tau_{t}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}`$ is a stopping time with respect to the
filtration $`(\mathcal{H}_{t,i})_{i=0}^{n}`$ if
$`\{\tau_{t}\leq i\}\in\mathcal{H}_{t,i}`$ for every $`i=0,\ldots,n`$.
We then establish the following conditional validity result.

###### Theorem 1.

Fix any round $`t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}`$. Under
Assumption [1](#Thmassumption1 "Assumption 1. ‣ 5 Theoretical Analysis ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees"),
for any $`m=1,\ldots,n`$, the statistic $`M^{(\beta)}_{t,m}`$ defined in
([10](#S4.E10 "In 4.2 E-Value Construction with Overlap Correction ‣ 4 Trustworthy Recursive Updates with Statistical Testing ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees"))
satisfies

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathbb{E}[M^{(\beta)}_{t,m}\mid\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{pre}}_{t}]\leq 1,\quad\text{if }H_{t}^{0}\text{ is true}.
``` |  | (12) |

This inequality also holds with $`m`$ replaced by any stopping time
$`\tau_{t}\leq n`$ with respect to $`(\mathcal{H}_{t,m})_{m=0}^{n}`$.

Theorem [1](#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1. ‣ 5 Theoretical Analysis ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
shows that our test statistic satisfies the conditional validity
requirement for e-values described in the statistical literature ([Vovk
and Wang, 2021](#bib.bib33); [Ramdas et al., 2023](#bib.bib21)). More
importantly, validity at arbitrary stopping times $`\tau_{t}\leq n`$
justifies the acceptance rule in
Algorithm [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ 4.2 E-Value Construction with Overlap Correction ‣ 4 Trustworthy Recursive Updates with Statistical Testing ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees"),
which stops at
$`\tau_{t}=\min\bigl\{n,\min\{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}:M^{(\beta)}_{t,i}\geq 1/\alpha_{t}\}\bigr\}`$.
The conditional validity also extends to mixtures of evidence processes
using different betting fractions. See
Corollary [1](#Thmcorollary1 "Corollary 1. ‣ Combining betting strategies. ‣ B.3 Mixtures ‣ Appendix B Proof of Conditional Evidence Validity ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
in the appendix. This validity result provides the basis for the
following theorem on FER control in RSI.

###### Theorem 2.

Fix a horizon $`T\geq 1`$ and an FER budget $`q\in(0,1)`$. Under
Assumption [1](#Thmassumption1 "Assumption 1. ‣ 5 Theoretical Analysis ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees"),
the false evolution rate of
Algorithm [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ 4.2 E-Value Construction with Overlap Correction ‣ 4 Trustworthy Recursive Updates with Statistical Testing ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
satisfies

|     |                                            |     |      |
|-----|--------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \operatorname{FER}_{\varepsilon}(T)\leq q.
       ```                                         |     | (13) |

Theorem [2](#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2. ‣ 5 Theoretical Analysis ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
controls the expected proportion of unacceptable updates among all
accepted updates over the entire self-improvement process with $`T`$
rounds. The same bound in
([13](#S5.E13 "In Theorem 2. ‣ 5 Theoretical Analysis ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees"))
holds with $`T`$ replaced by any stopping time $`\tau\leq T`$ with
respect to the self-improvement history $`(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t=0}^{T}`$,
as established in
Appendix [C.2](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Outer stopping extension of Theorem ‣ Appendix C Proof of False Evolution Rate Control ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees").
Thus, the process may be stopped based on the observed self-improvement
history without losing FER control. In addition, the FER guarantee does
not rely on the LLM correctly identifying beneficial candidates. The LLM
uses context to allocate more FER budget to promising candidates and
less to unpromising ones, safely improving the detection of beneficial
updates.

Let $`I_{t}=\mathbf{1}\{\Delta_{t}>0\}`$, $`U_{T}=\sum_{t\leq T}I_{t}`$,
and $`S_{T}=\sum_{t\leq T}I_{t}A_{t}`$. The true evolution rate (TER) is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\operatorname{TER}(T)=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{S_{T}}{U_{T}\vee 1}\right],
``` |  | (14) |

the expected proportion of improving candidates that are accepted
([Gadbury et al., 2004](#bib.bib10)).

###### Assumption 2.

Fix $`T`$ and $`\varepsilon\in(0,1)`$, and let $`N,n\to\infty`$. Suppose
that the following conditions are satisfied: (a) There exists a
deterministic sequence $`r_{N,n}\to 0`$ such that
$`\mathbb{P}(U_{T}=0)\leq r_{N,n}`$. (b) $`n/N\to 0`$. (c) There exist
constants $`\eta_{-}`$ and $`\eta_{+}`$ such that, for $`t=1,\ldots,T`$
and $`i=1,\ldots,n`$, the betting fractions satisfy
$`0<\eta_{-}\leq\eta_{t,i}\leq\eta_{+}<2\varepsilon/(1+\varepsilon)`$.

Assumption [2](#Thmassumption2 "Assumption 2. ‣ 5 Theoretical Analysis ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")(a)
requires the probability that the proposer fails to generate any
improvement to vanish as the sample size grows. As $`n\to\infty`$, the
proposer also receives more historical feedback, making it more likely
to produce at least one effective improvement.
Assumption [2](#Thmassumption2 "Assumption 2. ‣ 5 Theoretical Analysis ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")(b)
controls overlap by requiring $`n/N\to 0`$, so the bias caused by
overlap becomes very small.
Assumption [2](#Thmassumption2 "Assumption 2. ‣ 5 Theoretical Analysis ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")(c)
can be satisfied in practice by choosing the betting fraction
$`\eta_{t,i}`$ within the specified range.

###### Theorem 3.

Under
Assumption [2](#Thmassumption2 "Assumption 2. ‣ 5 Theoretical Analysis ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees"),
there exists a constant $`\kappa>0`$ such that for sufficiently large
$`N`$ and $`n`$,

|     |                                                     |     |      |
|-----|-----------------------------------------------------|-----|------|
|     |
       ``` math
       \operatorname{TER}(T)\geq 1-r_{N,n}-Te^{-\kappa n}.
       ```                                                  |     | (15) |

Hence, $`\operatorname{TER}(T)\to 1`$ as $`N,n\to\infty`$.

Theorem [3](#Thmtheorem3 "Theorem 3. ‣ 5 Theoretical Analysis ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
provides a lower bound on TER through two terms: $`r_{N,n}`$ bounds the
probability that no improving candidate is proposed, while
$`Te^{-\kappa n}`$ bounds the contribution of gate misses. Both terms
vanish as the sample size grows with $`T`$ fixed. It also reveals a
theoretical role of noninferiority: when $`\varepsilon=0`$, the upper
bound $`2\varepsilon/(1+\varepsilon)`$ in
Assumption [2](#Thmassumption2 "Assumption 2. ‣ 5 Theoretical Analysis ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
is zero, then no positive betting fraction satisfies the condition.
Under this argument, a positive $`\varepsilon`$ is needed to obtain
$`\operatorname{TER}(T)\to 1`$.

We next evaluate the final incumbent through its gain $`G(T)`$ defined
in
([2](#S3.E2 "In 3 Problem Formulation ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")).
Let $`\mathcal{P}_{T}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}[(\Delta_{t})_{+}]`$
denote the expected total positive effect of the proposed candidates.

###### Theorem 4.

Under
Assumptions [1](#Thmassumption1 "Assumption 1. ‣ 5 Theoretical Analysis ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
and [2](#Thmassumption2 "Assumption 2. ‣ 5 Theoretical Analysis ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees"),
there exists a constant $`\kappa>0`$ such that for sufficiently large
$`N`$ and $`n`$,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle G(T)\geq{}`$ | $`\displaystyle\bigl(1-e^{-\kappa n}\bigr)\mathcal{P}_{T}-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\!\left[A_{t}\min\{(-\Delta_{t})_{+},\varepsilon\}+\alpha_{t}(-\Delta_{t}-\varepsilon)_{+}\right].`$ |  | (16) |

The first term shows that the loss of positive improvement caused by
statistical rejection vanishes exponentially with the validation sample
size. The second term quantifies the downside from the noninferiority
margin. The third term controls loss beyond the tolerance through the
allocated testing levels $`\alpha_{t}`$ and may be reduced when the LLM
uses historical context to assign smaller testing levels to candidates
with greater downside risk. Thus, TRUST asymptotically preserves
beneficial updates while keeping harmful acceptance risk under
statistical control. Additionally, to measure losses along the
trajectory, we define population maximum drawdown (MDD) as
$`\operatorname{MDD}(T)=\max_{0\leq r<s\leq T}\bigl(J(\Theta_{r})-J(\Theta_{s})\bigr)_{+}`$.
Appendix [E](#A5 "Appendix E Proof of the Gain Bound ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
derives an upper bound on expected MDD.

## 6 Numerical Experiments

We compare the performance gains and acceptance risks of different gates
in an editable self-improvement harness, and conduct ablation studies to
examine the effects of TRUST’s key parameters. Supplementary diagnostics
and case studies in
Appendices [I](#A9 "Appendix I Overfitting Study ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
and [G.4](#A7.SS4 "G.4 Case study for Greedy ‣ Appendix G Experiment Details ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
show that higher validation scores do not necessarily imply better test
performance.

Following modular agent design ([Shang et al., 2025](#bib.bib29); [Hu et
al., 2025](#bib.bib12)), our editable harness
$`\mathsf{H}_{t}=(\mathsf{R}_{t},\mathsf{P}_{t},\mathsf{M}_{t},\mathsf{W}_{t})`$
exposes 20 operators across four components: reasoning ([Zhou et al.,
2023](#bib.bib46); [Fernando et al., 2024](#bib.bib9)), routing ([Lewis
et al., 2020](#bib.bib14); [Liu et al., 2024](#bib.bib17)), memory
([Shinn et al., 2023](#bib.bib31); [Zhao et al., 2024](#bib.bib44)), and
workflow ([Zhang et al., 2025a](#bib.bib42)).
Appendix [F](#A6 "Appendix F Harness Design with Four Components and Operators ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
details operator designs.

### 6.1 Main gate comparison

#### Datasets, models and metrics.

We evaluate the benchmarks HotpotQA ([Yang et al., 2018](#bib.bib39)),
DROP ([Dua et al., 2019](#bib.bib7)), and APPS ([Hendrycks et al.,
2021](#bib.bib11)) with the models Qwen-3.8-Flash and DeepSeek-V4-Flash.
For each dataset, the validation sets contain a total of 200 examples,
with 300 held-out examples used only as the test set. We use additional
20 examples to construct the initial information $`\mathcal{F}_{0}`$.
Test scores are measured by Joint F1 for HotpotQA, official token F1 for
DROP, and the test case pass rate for APPS. We report gain and MDD in
percentage points (pp). For a stricter empirical diagnostic, we report
$`\mathrm{FER}_{0}`$, which counts all accepted non-improving updates.

#### Baselines.

We compare TRUST with four baselines over 30 rounds and 5 seeds. Greedy
accepts candidates with higher scores on the validation samples. PACE
uses an e-process test to control false acceptance within a single round
([Shawn, 2026](#bib.bib41)). SGM-B and SGM-H use Bernstein and Hoeffding
tests, respectively, with FWER control across rounds ([Wu et al.,
2025](#bib.bib37)). For TRUST, we set $`q=0.30`$, $`\varepsilon=0.10`$,
and $`\beta=0.01`$. More implementation details appear in
Appendix [G](#A7 "Appendix G Experiment Details ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees").

Table 1: Main comparison: DeepSeek-V4-Flash (left) and Qwen-3.8-Flash
(right). Values are mean $`\pm`$ standard error. Methods with empirical
$`\mathrm{FER}_{0}>30\%`$ are shown in gray to indicate high risk. Bold
marks the best Gain, TER, and MDD results among the remaining methods
within each setting.

TRUST achieves high scores in all six settings and the highest scores in
four of them
(Table [1](#S6.T1 "Table 1 ‣ Baselines. ‣ 6.1 Main gate comparison ‣ 6 Numerical Experiments ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")).
It maintains FER₀ below 30% in all six settings, whereas Greedy exceeds
30% in every setting. The highest MDD for TRUST is only 0.27 pp, whereas
Greedy reaches 8.48 pp.
Appendix [G.4](#A7.SS4 "G.4 Case study for Greedy ‣ Appendix G Experiment Details ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
presents a case study of Greedy that improves validation performance but
harms test performance.

Among the remaining methods with $`\mathrm{FER}_{0}\leq 30\%`$, SGM-B
never accepts an update and SGM-H accepts only three updates across all
runs, so they show no score improvement in most settings. These results
suggest that both methods for FWER control across multiple rounds are
conservative in practice. Relative to PACE, TRUST achieves higher scores
and TER in all six settings, with FER₀ lower in one setting, equal in
three, and higher in two.

Figure 4: Left: mean gain trajectories. Right: mean final gain versus
mean MDD, with shading marking the 1 pp empirical MDD reference region.
Results are averaged across six settings.

The left panel of
Figure [4](#S6.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ Baselines. ‣ 6.1 Main gate comparison ‣ 6 Numerical Experiments ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
shows the mean gain trajectories over rounds. Greedy achieves rapid
initial gains but exhibits subsequent declines. TRUST overtakes Greedy
in later rounds and preserves its accumulated gains, achieving the
highest final mean gain. The right panel compares mean final gain and
mean MDD. We mark the region with mean MDD below 1 pp as an empirical
safe area. TRUST lies within this region while achieving the highest
among all compared methods, striking a balance between performance gain
and stability in RSI.

### 6.2 Ablation study

We next conduct ablation studies on HotpotQA/DeepSeek-V4-Flash. We vary
three key parameters: the FER budget $`q`$, noninferiority margin
$`\varepsilon`$, and overlap correction strength $`\beta`$. We also
compare TRUST with and without LLM guidance.
Appendix [H](#A8 "Appendix H Supplementary Sensitivity Analysis ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
provides the detailed settings and results.

Figure 5: Bars show mean gain, and lines show FER₀ and TER. We vary one
parameter at a time, keeping the others at the default values
$`(q,\varepsilon,\beta)=(0.30,0.10,0.01)`$.

Figure 6: Mean gain trajectories with and without LLM allocation.

Figure [5](#S6.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ 6.2 Ablation study ‣ 6 Numerical Experiments ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
shows that larger $`q`$ and $`\varepsilon`$ make TRUST more aggressive,
with higher TER and FER₀. Larger $`\beta`$ makes it more conservative:
FER₀ drops from 23.33% at $`\beta=0`$ to 0% at $`\beta=0.03`$. These
trends are consistent with the definitions of the three parameters. In
each ablation study, the gain first increases, then decreases or holds
steady. For $`\beta`$, it peaks at 11.58 pp when $`\beta=0.01`$,
compared with 10.97 pp without correction, suggesting that an
appropriate overlap correction strength can be beneficial in practice.
Appendix [H.1](#A8.SS1 "H.1 Parameter sensitivity analysis ‣ Appendix H Supplementary Sensitivity Analysis ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
provides additional trajectory results and a more detailed analysis.
These results highlight the importance of balancing conservative and
aggressive acceptance to improve performance.

We replace the LLM allocator with the default multiplier
$`w_{t}\equiv 1`$ while keeping other parameters in TRUST unchanged.
Figure [6](#S6.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ 6.2 Ablation study ‣ 6 Numerical Experiments ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
shows that TRUST achieves a higher final gain, corresponding to final
scores of 44.91 $`\pm`$ 1.36% for TRUST and 43.78 $`\pm`$ 1.18% for
TRUST without LLM guidance. This suggests a performance benefit from
using context to guide the allocation of testing levels. We provide a
detailed case study in
Appendix [H.2](#A8.SS2 "H.2 LLM allocator analysis ‣ Appendix H Supplementary Sensitivity Analysis ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
showing how the LLM analyzes context and assigns different LLM
multipliers to influence acceptance decisions.

## 7 Conclusion and Limitations

TRUST applies online multiple hypothesis testing to control FER across
rounds in self-improving agents, with LLM guidance and interpretable
parameters for adjusting gate behavior. Our theoretical analysis and
experiments show that the framework helps balance useful improvement and
acceptance error control under limited validation resources. It extends
existing statistical methods and provides a flexible statistical
foundation for future work on recursive self-improvement. We discuss
this point further in
Appendix [A.2](#A1.SS2 "A.2 Relation to Existing Statistical Methods ‣ Appendix A Supplementary Discussion ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees").
Overall, we show that TRUST strikes a balance between statistical
reliability and performance gains in recursive self-improvement.

The present study does not address how to identify an exact value of
overlap correction strength from data or how to optimally tune other
parameters. The noninferiority margin provides an interpretable
tolerance for loss, but the theoretical guarantee can be conservative
and only partly explains practical behavior. The LLM allocator can
occasionally make mistakes when judging candidate updates, which may
lead to poor choices of LLM multipliers. Optimizing the gate to maximize
gain while keeping safety metrics within target ranges is also a
promising direction. Future work can address these limitations to make
the recursive self-improvement process more trustworthy and effective.
````
