You’ll have a function called "\*\*sortEmotions\*\*" that will return an
array of \*\*emotions\*\* sorted. It has two parameters, the first
parameter called "\*\*arr\*\*" will expect an array of \*\*emotions\*\*
where an \*\*emotion\*\* will be one of the following:

- \*\*:D\*\* -\> Super Happy

- \*\*:)\*\* -\> Happy

- \*\*:\|\*\* -\> Normal

- \*\*:(\*\* -\> Sad

- \*\*T\\\_T\*\* -\> Super Sad

Example of the array:‘‘\[ ’T_T’, ’:D’, ’:\|’, ’:)’, ’:(’ \]‘‘

And the second parameter is called "\*\*order\*\*", if this parameter is
\*\*true\*\* then the order of the emotions will be descending (from
\*\*Super Happy\*\* to \*\*Super Sad\*\*) if it’s \*\*false\*\* then it
will be ascending (from \*\*Super Sad\*\* to \*\*Super Happy\*\*)

Example if \*\*order\*\* is true with the above array: ‘‘\[ ’:D’, ’:)’,
’:\|’, ’:(’, ’T_T’ \]‘‘

- Super Happy -\> Happy -\> Normal -\> Sad -\> Super Sad

If \*\*order\*\* is false: ‘‘\[ ’T_T’, ’:(’, ’:\|’, ’:)’, ’:D’ \]‘‘

- Super Sad -\> Sad -\> Normal -\> Happy -\> Super Happy

Example:

‘‘‘

arr = \[’:D’, ’:\|’, ’:)’, ’:(’, ’:D’\]

sortEmotions(arr, true) // \[ ’:D’, ’:D’, ’:)’, ’:\|’, ’:(’ \]

sortEmotions(arr, false) // \[ ’:(’, ’:\|’, ’:)’, ’:D’, ’:D’ \]

‘‘‘

The Emotional Sort problem asks the solver to write a function that
sorts a list of emotion symbols by their happiness levels. The symbols
have a fixed order from happiest to saddest: :D, :), :\|, :(, and T_T.
The parameter order determines the sorting direction: true requires the
happiest emotions first, while false requires the saddest emotions
first.

[⬇](data:text/plain;base64,ZGVmIHNvcnRfZW1vdGlvbnMoYXJyLCBvcmRlcik6CiAgICAjIERlZmluZSB0aGUgb3JkZXIgb2YgZW1vdGlvbnMgZnJvbSBTdXBlciBIYXBweSB0byBTdXBlciBTYWQKICAgIGVtb3Rpb25fb3JkZXIgPSBbJzpEJywgJzopJywgJzp8JywgJzooJywgJ1RfVCddCgogICAgIyBDcmVhdGUgYSBtYXBwaW5nIGZyb20gZW1vdGlvbiB0byBpdHMgcmFuawogICAgcmFuayA9IHtlbW90aW9uOiBpIGZvciBpLCBlbW90aW9uIGluIGVudW1lcmF0ZShlbW90aW9uX29yZGVyKX0KCiAgICAjIFNvcnQgdGhlIGFycmF5IGJhc2VkIG9uIHRoZSByYW5rCiAgICAjIElmIG9yZGVyIGlzIFRydWUsIHNvcnQgZGVzY2VuZGluZyAoU3VwZXIgSGFwcHkgZmlyc3QpCiAgICAjIElmIG9yZGVyIGlzIEZhbHNlLCBzb3J0IGFzY2VuZGluZyAoU3VwZXIgU2FkIGZpcnN0KQogICAgc29ydGVkX2FyciA9IHNvcnRlZChhcnIsIGtleT1sYW1iZGEgeDogcmFua1t4XSwgcmV2ZXJzZT1vcmRlcikKCiAgICByZXR1cm4gc29ydGVkX2Fycg==)

def sort_emotions(arr, order):

\# Define the order of emotions from Super Happy to Super Sad

emotion_order = \[’:D’, ’:)’, ’:\|’, ’:(’, ’T_T’\]

\# Create a mapping from emotion to its rank

rank = {emotion: i for i, emotion in enumerate(emotion_order)}

\# Sort the array based on the rank

\# If order is True, sort descending (Super Happy first)

\# If order is False, sort ascending (Super Sad first)

sorted_arr = sorted(arr, key=lambda x: rank\[x\], reverse=order)

return sorted_arr

The incumbent assigns rank $`0`$ to the happiest emotion and rank $`4`$
to the saddest, but uses reverse=order. When order=True, this places the
saddest emotions first, contrary to the task requirement. The candidate
corrects the sorting direction: it sorts by increasing rank when
order=True and by decreasing rank when order=False. The incumbent
response incorrectly treats order=True, which requests the happiest
emotions first, as reverse=True, which places the largest rank first and
therefore puts the saddest emotions first.

[⬇](data:text/plain;base64,ZGVmIHNvcnRfZW1vdGlvbnMoYXJyLCBvcmRlcik6CiAgICAjIERlZmluZSB0aGUgb3JkZXIgb2YgZW1vdGlvbnMgZnJvbSBTdXBlciBIYXBweSB0byBTdXBlciBTYWQKICAgIGVtb3Rpb25fb3JkZXIgPSBbJzpEJywgJzopJywgJzp8JywgJzooJywgJ1RfVCddCgogICAgIyBJZiBvcmRlciBpcyB0cnVlLCBzb3J0IGRlc2NlbmRpbmcgKFN1cGVyIEhhcHB5IGZpcnN0KQogICAgIyBJZiBvcmRlciBpcyBmYWxzZSwgc29ydCBhc2NlbmRpbmcgKFN1cGVyIFNhZCBmaXJzdCkKICAgICMgVXNlIHRoZSBpbmRleCBpbiBlbW90aW9uX29yZGVyIGFzIHRoZSBzb3J0IGtleQogICAgIyBGb3IgZGVzY2VuZGluZywgc29ydCBieSBuZWdhdGl2ZSBpbmRleDsgZm9yIGFzY2VuZGluZywgc29ydCBieSBpbmRleAogICAgaWYgb3JkZXI6CiAgICAgICAgcmV0dXJuIHNvcnRlZChhcnIsIGtleT1sYW1iZGEgZTogZW1vdGlvbl9vcmRlci5pbmRleChlKSkKICAgIGVsc2U6CiAgICAgICAgcmV0dXJuIHNvcnRlZChhcnIsIGtleT1sYW1iZGEgZTogZW1vdGlvbl9vcmRlci5pbmRleChlKSwgcmV2ZXJzZT1UcnVlKQ==)

def sort_emotions(arr, order):

\# Define the order of emotions from Super Happy to Super Sad

emotion_order = \[’:D’, ’:)’, ’:\|’, ’:(’, ’T_T’\]

\# If order is true, sort descending (Super Happy first)

\# If order is false, sort ascending (Super Sad first)

\# Use the index in emotion_order as the sort key

\# For descending, sort by negative index; for ascending, sort by index

if order:

return sorted(arr, key=lambda e: emotion_order.index(e))

else:

return sorted(arr, key=lambda e: emotion_order.index(e), reverse=True)

The candidate corrects the branch logic. It sorts by increasing index
when order is true and reverses the order when it is false, matching the
problem’s specification.

[⬇](data:text/plain;base64,ewogICJhY2NlcHRlZCI6IHRydWUsCiAgIm91dGVyX2VmZmVjdF9nZW9tZXRyeSI6IHsKICAgICJzYW1wbGVfc2l6ZSI6IDQwLAogICAgIm1lYW5fZGlmZmVyZW5jZSI6IDAuMDQsCiAgICAiY2FuZGlkYXRlX3dpbnMiOiA0LAogICAgImluY3VtYmVudF93aW5zIjogMiwKICAgICJ0aWVzIjogMzQsCiAgICAibWluaW11bSI6IC0xLjAsCiAgICAibWF4aW11bSI6IDEuMCwKICAgICJlc3RpbWFuZCI6ICJtZWFuX3Blcl9wcm9ibGVtX3N1cHBsaWVkX3Rlc3RfcGFzc19mcmFjdGlvbiIKICB9LAogICJnYXRlX2RlY2lzaW9uIjogewogICAgInBvbGljeSI6ICJwYWlyZWRfbWVhbl9hcHBzX3Byb2JsZW1fcGFzc19mcmFjdGlvbl9ncmVlZHkiLAogICAgInRlc3RfaW5kZXgiOiAxNiwKICAgICJtZWFuX2RpZmZlcmVuY2UiOiAwLjA0LAogICAgImVzdGltYW5kIjogIm1lYW5fcGVyX3Byb2JsZW1fc3VwcGxpZWRfdGVzdF9wYXNzX2ZyYWN0aW9uIgogIH0KfQ==)

{

"accepted": true,

"outer_effect_geometry": {

"mean_difference": 0.04,

"candidate_wins": 4,

"ties": 34,

"minimum": -1.0,

"maximum": 1.0,

"estimand": "mean_per_problem_supplied_test_pass_fraction"

},

"gate_decision": {

"policy": "paired_mean_apps_problem_pass_fraction_greedy",

"test_index": 16,

"mean_difference": 0.04,

"estimand": "mean_per_problem_supplied_test_pass_fraction"

}

}

Four problem scores improve, two decline, and 34 stay unchanged. Besides
Emotional Sort, two graph problems improve from invalid responses to
4/5. These gains are accompanied by two complete regressions. The mean
score rises from 60.50% to 64.50% on the validation set, so Greedy
accepts the candidate.

We next examine a test problem about the number of days off in a Martian
year.

[⬇](data:text/plain;base64,T24gdGhlIHBsYW5ldCBNYXJzIGEgeWVhciBsYXN0cyBleGFjdGx5IG4gZGF5cyAodGhlcmUgYXJlIG5vIGxlYXAgeWVhcnMgb24gTWFycykuIEJ1dCBNYXJ0aWFucyBoYXZlIHRoZSBzYW1lIHdlZWtzIGFzIGVhcnRobGluZ3PigJQgNSB3b3JrIGRheXMgYW5kIHRoZW4gMiBkYXlzIG9mZi4gWW91ciB0YXNrIGlzIHRvIGRldGVybWluZSB0aGUgbWluaW11bSBwb3NzaWJsZSBhbmQgdGhlIG1heGltdW0gcG9zc2libGUgbnVtYmVyIG9mIGRheXMgb2ZmIHBlciB5ZWFyIG9uIE1hcnMuCgotLS0tLUlucHV0LS0tLS0KClRoZSBmaXJzdCBsaW5lIG9mIHRoZSBpbnB1dCBjb250YWlucyBhIHBvc2l0aXZlIGludGVnZXIgbiAoMSDiiaQgbiDiiaQgMSAwMDAgMDAwKSDigJQgdGhlIG51bWJlciBvZiBkYXlzIGluIGEgeWVhciBvbiBNYXJzLgoKLS0tLS1PdXRwdXQtLS0tLQoKUHJpbnQgdHdvIGludGVnZXJzwqDigJQgdGhlIG1pbmltdW0gcG9zc2libGUgYW5kIHRoZSBtYXhpbXVtIHBvc3NpYmxlIG51bWJlciBvZiBkYXlzIG9mZiBwZXIgeWVhciBvbiBNYXJzLgoKLS0tLS1FeGFtcGxlcy0tLS0tCklucHV0CjE0Ck91dHB1dAo0IDQKCklucHV0CjIKT3V0cHV0CjAgMg==)

On the planet Mars a year lasts exactly n days (there are no leap years
on Mars). But Martians have the same weeks as earthlings— 5 work days
and then 2 days off. Your task is to determine the minimum possible and
the maximum possible number of days off per year on Mars.

-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a positive integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 1 000
000) — the number of days in a year on Mars.

-----Output-----

Print two integers — the minimum possible and the maximum possible
number of days off per year on Mars.

-----Examples-----

Input

14

Output

4 4

Input

2

Output

0 2

A full seven-day week contains two days off. For $`n=7w+r`$, with
$`0\leq r<7`$, the required minimum and maximum are $`2w+\max(0,r-5)`$
and $`2w+\min(r,2)`$. The first example has no remainder, while the
second directly tests a partial week.

The number of days off depends on which day of the week the year begins.
Every complete seven-day period contains exactly two days off, while any
remaining days may include additional days off. For $`n=14`$, the year
contains exactly two full weeks, so both the minimum and maximum are
four. For $`n=2`$, the two days could both be workdays or both be days
off, giving a minimum of zero and a maximum of two. The incumbent and
candidate receive the same complete problem statement, including both
examples.

[⬇](data:text/plain;base64,CmRlZiBtYWluKCk6CiAgICBkYXRhID0gc3lzLnN0ZGluLnJlYWQoKS5zdHJpcCgpLnNwbGl0KCkKICAgIGlmIG5vdCBkYXRhOgogICAgICAgIHJldHVybgogICAgbiA9IGludChkYXRhWzBdKQogICAgZnVsbF93ZWVrcyA9IG4gLy8gNwogICAgcmVtID0gbiAlIDcKICAgIG1pbl9vZmYgPSBmdWxsX3dlZWtzICogMgogICAgbWF4X29mZiA9IGZ1bGxfd2Vla3MgKiAyCiAgICBtaW5fb2ZmID0gZnVsbF93ZWVrcyAqIDIgKyBtYXgoMCwgcmVtIC0gNSkKICAgIG1heF9vZmYgPSBmdWxsX3dlZWtzICogMiArIG1pbihyZW0sIDIpCiAgICBwcmludChtaW5fb2ZmLCBtYXhfb2ZmKQoKaWYgX19uYW1lX18gPT0gIl9fbWFpbl9fIjoKICAgIG1haW4oKQ==)

def main():

data = sys.stdin.read().strip().split()

if not data:

return

n = int(data\[0\])

full_weeks = n // 7

rem = n % 7

min_off = full_weeks \* 2

max_off = full_weeks \* 2

min_off = full_weeks \* 2 + max(0, rem - 5)

max_off = full_weeks \* 2 + min(rem, 2)

print(min_off, max_off)

if \_\_name\_\_ == "\_\_main\_\_":

main()

The incumbent counts two days off for each full week and then accounts
for the remaining days. In particular, min(rem, 2) adds the maximum
possible number of days off among those remaining days. This program
passes all five evaluation test cases.

[⬇](data:text/plain;base64,CmRlZiBtYWluKCk6CiAgICBkYXRhID0gc3lzLnN0ZGluLnJlYWQoKS5zdHJpcCgpLnNwbGl0KCkKICAgIGlmIG5vdCBkYXRhOgogICAgICAgIHJldHVybgogICAgbiA9IGludChkYXRhWzBdKQogICAgZnVsbF93ZWVrcyA9IG4gLy8gNwogICAgcmVtID0gbiAlIDcKICAgIG1pbl9vZmYgPSBmdWxsX3dlZWtzICogMgogICAgbWF4X29mZiA9IGZ1bGxfd2Vla3MgKiAyCiAgICBtaW5fb2ZmICs9IG1heCgwLCByZW0gLSA1KQogICAgcHJpbnQobWluX29mZiwgbWF4X29mZikKCmlmIF9fbmFtZV9fID09ICJfX21haW5fXyI6CiAgICBtYWluKCk=)

def main():

data = sys.stdin.read().strip().split()

if not data:

return

n = int(data\[0\])

full_weeks = n // 7

rem = n % 7

min_off = full_weeks \* 2

max_off = full_weeks \* 2

min_off += max(0, rem - 5)

print(min_off, max_off)

if \_\_name\_\_ == "\_\_main\_\_":

main()

The candidate correctly computes the minimum number of days off, but
counts only complete weeks when computing the maximum, ignoring possible
days off in the remaining days. For the first example, $`n=14`$, there
are no remaining days, so the candidate still returns the correct
output, 4 4. For the second example, $`n=2`$, there are no full weeks,
so it incorrectly returns 0 0 instead of 0 2. This behavior is
consistent with the first_example_pass instruction: the first supplied
example does not show this error, while the second does. Finally, the
candidate passes only one of the five evaluation test cases, compared
with all five for the incumbent.

[⬇](data:text/plain;base64,ewogICJtZXRyaWNzIjogewogICAgIml0ZW1zIjogMzAwLAogICAgIm1lYW5fcGVyX3Byb2JsZW1fdGVzdF9wYXNzX2ZyYWN0aW9uIjogMC43NTA2NjY2NjY2NjY2NjY2LAogICAgInNjaGVtYV9wYXJzZV9mYWlsdXJlcyI6IDM3LAogICAgInN1cHBsaWVkX3Rlc3RzX3Bhc3NlZCI6IDExMjYsCiAgICAic3VwcGxpZWRfdGVzdHNfdG90YWwiOiAxNTAwCiAgfQp9)

{

"metrics": {

"items": 300,

"mean_per_problem_test_pass_fraction": 0.7506666666666666,

"schema_parse_failures": 37,

"supplied_tests_passed": 1126,

"supplied_tests_total": 1500

}

}

[⬇](data:text/plain;base64,ewogICJtZXRyaWNzIjogewogICAgIml0ZW1zIjogMzAwLAogICAgIm1lYW5fcGVyX3Byb2JsZW1fdGVzdF9wYXNzX2ZyYWN0aW9uIjogMC42NywKICAgICJzY2hlbWFfcGFyc2VfZmFpbHVyZXMiOiA2MCwKICAgICJzdXBwbGllZF90ZXN0c19wYXNzZWQiOiAxMDA1LAogICAgInN1cHBsaWVkX3Rlc3RzX3RvdGFsIjogMTUwMAogIH0KfQ==)

{

Across all 300 test problems, the mean fraction of passed test cases
falls from $`75.07\%`$ for the incumbent to $`67.00\%`$ for the
candidate. Although the update changes only one workflow instruction, it
changes the solver’s stopping criterion and may encourage it to focus on
matching the first supplied example. In the Martian year problem, the
first supplied example contains only complete weeks, so matching it does
not require correct handling of the remaining days. The candidate’s
missing calculation illustrates how a program can satisfy this narrower
requirement while failing other cases. In this case, the update improves
validation performance but reduces performance on the held-out test
problems.

reported one-parameter grids are $`q\in\{0.1,0.3,0.5\}`$,

$`\beta\in\{0,0.01,0.03,0.05\}`$, around
$`(q,\varepsilon,\beta)=(0.30,0.10,0.01)`$.

In
Figure [7](#A8.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ H.1 Parameter sensitivity analysis ‣ Appendix H Supplementary Sensitivity Analysis ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees"),
the parameters affect how much performance improves and whether that
improvement lasts. With $`q=0.1`$, the mean trajectory first rises at
round 9 and reaches a lower final score; $`q=0.3`$ improves earlier,
whereas $`q=0.5`$ shows a late change in the acceptance decision without
a higher final score. For the margin, $`\varepsilon=0`$ produces no
accepted updates, and $`\varepsilon=0.05`$ delays improvement until
round 21. Increasing the margin $`\varepsilon`$ to 0.15 yields early
gains but subsequent fluctuations. MDD rises from 0.02 pp to 5.33 pp.
For the overlap correction strength, $`\beta=0`$ shows a late decline,
while $`\beta=0.01`$ retains a similar peak with little subsequent loss.
Larger correction strengths of 0.03 and 0.05 reduce gains to 7.13 pp and
2.80 pp.

### H.2 LLM allocator analysis

This section provides a case study to supplement the LLM ablation study
in
Section [6.2](#S6.SS2 "6.2 Ablation study ‣ 6 Numerical Experiments ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees").
We examine HotpotQA/DeepSeek-V4-Flash with seed 3, comparing TRUST with
an ablation that replaces the LLM allocator with a fixed multiplier
$`w_{t}=1`$. The proposer, solver and statistical testing are unchanged.
Historical information offers identical parent states, candidates,
results and evidence before round 2. Both methods reject in round 1, and
their acceptance decisions first diverge in round 2.

It proposes breaking questions that involve multiple entities into

assessment, it recommends a very conservative allocation. The excerpt
below shows only selected fields of the assessment and the full

The allocator combines the LLM scores with the very_conservative
recommendation to determine the LLM multiplier and testing level. This
produces a stricter acceptance threshold than the baseline with
$`w_{t}\equiv 1`$. Given the same validation evidence, TRUST rejects the
candidate while the baseline accepts it, leading to different subsequent
search trajectories. Although the rejected candidate also improves on
the initial incumbent, TRUST later accepts a candidate with a larger
gain on the test set. For this seed, TRUST finishes at Joint F1 47.22%,
compared with 43.95% for the baseline. This case illustrates how the LLM
module can influence allocation and subsequent search through
assessments of each candidate and its context. These assessments guide
the testing level within the statistical gate, preserving FER control.

## Appendix I Overfitting Study

Here, we analyze an overfitting stress test based on our harness design
in
Appendix [F](#A6 "Appendix F Harness Design with Four Components and Operators ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees"),
using Greedy as the acceptance gate.
Figure [8](#A9.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ Appendix I Overfitting Study ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
shows the mean gain trajectories of the Greedy gate on HotpotQA
Distractor using DeepSeek-V4-Flash and Joint F1 over solver seeds
$`s=0,\ldots,4`$ and data-split seeds $`42+s`$. Each run reuses 20 fixed
examples for proposing and validation and the test set contains 100
examples. Each round proposes one routing or memory update using closed
historical feedback and accepts it only if Joint F1 improves on the
fixed validation set. Reasoning and workflow remain frozen.

Figure 8: Mean validation and test gain trajectories during recursive
self-improvement.

#### Mechanism and interpretation.

Routing selects the evidence paragraphs shown to the solver for the
current question, while memory provides retrieved cases and feedback
from previous tasks. Their interaction involves both a shared context
budget and different retrieval modes, which affect which evidence
paragraphs are selected and how many are retained. This design is
motivated by work on retrieval and context utilization ([Shi et al.,
2024](#bib.bib30); [Shinn et al., 2023](#bib.bib31); [Zhao et al.,
2024](#bib.bib44); [Liu et al., 2024](#bib.bib17)).

Figure [8](#A9.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ Appendix I Overfitting Study ‣ Trustworthy Recursive Self-Improvement with Statistical Guarantees")
shows that mean validation gain reaches 24.44 pp as the same data are
reused for proposing and validation. Relative to round 0, mean test gain
peaks at 5.06 pp after round 1, then falls to -2.15 pp by round 10. This
divergence suggests that iterative harness optimization can overfit
reused proposal and validation data.

### I.1 Case study for overfitting

On the HotpotQA benchmark ([Yang et al., 2018](#bib.bib39)), a system
answers questions that require combining information from multiple
Wikipedia articles. It must also identify the supporting facts, namely
the specific sentences in the provided paragraphs that together justify
the answer. We present a concrete example below.

[⬇](data:text/plain;base64,V2hhdCBpcyB0aGUgbmFtZSBvZiB0aGUgbGVhZCByZW1peCBvZiB0aGUgdHJhY2sgZnJvbSB0aGUgcmVtaXggYWxidW0gY29udGFpbmluZyByZW1peGVzIG9mIHRoZSBsYWR5IGdhZ2Egc3R1ZGlvIGFsYnVtICJUaGUgRmFtZSI/)

What is the name of the lead remix of the track from the remix album
containing remixes of the lady gaga studio album "The Fame"?

The correct answer is Bad Romance. The supporting facts establish two
connections. First, The Remix contains remixes of songs from Lady Gaga’s
studio album The Fame. Second, Starsmith’s remix of Bad Romance was
selected as the lead remix on The Remix. Before the update, the
incumbent retrieves no case memory and selects eight of the ten
available paragraphs, including the required sources.

[⬇](data:text/plain;base64,ewogICJmaW5hbF9hbnN3ZXIiOiAiU3RhcnNtaXRoIFJlbWl4IiwKICAic3VwcG9ydGluZ19mYWN0cyI6IFsKICAgIHsidGl0bGUiOiAiU3RhcnNtaXRoIiwgInNlbnRlbmNlX2lkIjogNH0KICBdLAogICJhdG9taWNfZmFjdHMiOiBbCiAgICB7InN1YmplY3QiOiAiU3RhcnNtaXRoIiwgInJlbGF0aW9uIjogInJlbWl4ZWQiLAogICAgICJvYmplY3QiOiAiQmFkIFJvbWFuY2UiLAogICAgICJ0aXRsZSI6ICJTdGFyc21pdGgiLCAic2VudGVuY2VfaWQiOiA0fSwKICAgIHsic3ViamVjdCI6ICJTdGFyc21pdGgncyByZW1peCBvZiBCYWQgUm9tYW5jZSIsICJyZWxhdGlvbiI6ICJ3YXMgY2hvc2VuIGFzIGxlYWQgcmVtaXggb24iLAogICAgICJvYmplY3QiOiAiVGhlIFJlbWl4IiwKICAgICAidGl0bGUiOiAiU3RhcnNtaXRoIiwgInNlbnRlbmNlX2lkIjogNH0KICBdLAogICJyZWFzb25pbmciOiB7Im9wZXJhdGlvbiI6ICJicmlkZ2UiLCAiZmFjdF9pbmRpY2VzIjogWzAsIDFdLAogICAgICAgICAgICAgICAgInJlc3VsdCI6ICJTdGFyc21pdGggUmVtaXgifQp9)

{

"final_answer": "Starsmith Remix",

"supporting_facts": \[

{"title": "Starsmith", "sentence_id": 4}

\],

"atomic_facts": \[

{"subject": "Starsmith", "relation": "remixed",

"object": "Bad Romance",

"title": "Starsmith", "sentence_id": 4},

{"subject": "Starsmith’s remix of Bad Romance", "relation": "was chosen
as lead remix on",

"object": "The Remix",

"title": "Starsmith", "sentence_id": 4}

\],

"reasoning": {"operation": "bridge", "fact_indices": \[0, 1\],

"result": "Starsmith Remix"}

}

The response identifies the remix relationship but returns Starsmith
Remix as the answer. It also omits sentence 1 of The Remix (Lady Gaga
album) from its supporting facts. The answer is incorrect, and Joint F1
is 0. The proposer reflects on this failure and proposes adding the
following episodic memory.

[⬇](data:text/plain;base64,ewogICJtZW1vcnlfaWQiOiAibWVtLWY1MmM5YzU0MGE2MCIsCiAgInF1ZXN0aW9uX3RleHQiOiAiV2hhdCBpcyB0aGUgbmFtZSBvZiB0aGUgbGVhZCByZW1peCBvZiB0aGUgdHJhY2sgZnJvbSB0aGUgcmVtaXggYWxidW0gY29udGFpbmluZyByZW1peGVzIG9mIHRoZSBsYWR5IGdhZ2Egc3R1ZGlvIGFsYnVtIFwiVGhlIEZhbWVcIj8iLAogICJ2ZXJpZmllZF9hbnN3ZXIiOiAiQmFkIFJvbWFuY2UiLAogICJzdXBwb3J0X2ZhY3RzIjogW1siU3RhcnNtaXRoIiwgNF0sIFsiVGhlIFJlbWl4IChMYWR5IEdhZ2EgYWxidW0pIiwgMV1dLAogICJxdWVyeV90b2tlbnMiOiBbCiAgICAiYWxidW0iLAogICAgImNvbnRhaW5pbmciLAogICAgImZhbWUiLAogICAgImdhZ2EiLAogICAgImxhZHkiLAogICAgImxlYWQiLAogICAgIm5hbWUiLAogICAgInJlbWl4IiwKICAgICJyZW1peGVzIiwKICAgICJzdHVkaW8iLAogICAgInRyYWNrIgogIF0sCiAgImNsdXN0ZXJfdGhyZXNob2xkIjogMC4wMSwKICAicHJvY2VkdXJlIjogWwogICAgIlJldXNlIGEgdmVyaWZpZWQgYW5zd2VyIG9ubHkgd2hlbiB0aGUgY3VycmVudCBxdWVzdGlvbiBpcyBpZGVudGljYWwuIiwKICAgICJGb3IgYSBub24taWRlbnRpY2FsIGNsdXN0ZXIgbWF0Y2gsIHRyZWF0IHRoZSBlcGlzb2RlIG9ubHkgYXMgYW4gYW5hbG9neSBhbmQgdmVyaWZ5IGJvdGggYnJpZGdlIGhvcHMuIgogIF0sCiAgInN1cHBvcnRpbmdfZmFjdF9wb2xpY3kiOiAiUmV0dXJuIG9ubHkgc3VwcG9ydGluZyBmYWN0cyBncm91bmRlZCBpbiB0aGUgY3VycmVudCBXaWtpcGVkaWEgY29udGV4dC4iCn0=)

{

"memory_id": "mem-f52c9c540a60",

"question_text": "What is the name of the lead remix of the track from
the remix album containing remixes of the lady gaga studio album \\The
Fame\\?",

"verified_answer": "Bad Romance",

"support_facts": \[\["Starsmith", 4\], \["The Remix (Lady Gaga album)",
1\]\],

"query_tokens": \[

"album",

"containing",

"fame",

"gaga",

"lady",

"lead",

"name",

"remix",

"remixes",

"studio",

"track"

\],

"cluster_threshold": 0.01,

"procedure": \[

"Reuse a verified answer only when the current question is identical.",

"For a non-identical cluster match, treat the episode only as an analogy
and verify both bridge hops."

\],

"supporting_fact_policy": "Return only supporting facts grounded in the
current Wikipedia context."

}

This episode stores the full validation question, its verified answer,
and both supporting-fact locations. Its procedure permits answer reuse
for an identical question. If a different question retrieves this
memory, the solver is instructed to analyze the question using both the
retrieved memory and the current Wikipedia context, and to verify that
both reasoning steps are supported by evidence in that context.
Retrieval compares the words in the current question with those in the
stored question.

For the repeated validation question, the system retrieves the stored
memory as an exact match and supplies its verified answer and supporting
fact locations to the solver. The selected context still includes both
required evidence sources.

[⬇](data:text/plain;base64,ewogICJmaW5hbF9hbnN3ZXIiOiAiQmFkIFJvbWFuY2UiLAogICJzdXBwb3J0aW5nX2ZhY3RzIjogWwogICAgeyJ0aXRsZSI6ICJTdGFyc21pdGgiLCAic2VudGVuY2VfaWQiOiA0fSwKICAgIHsidGl0bGUiOiAiVGhlIFJlbWl4IChMYWR5IEdhZ2EgYWxidW0pIiwgInNlbnRlbmNlX2lkIjogMX0KICBdLAogICJhdG9taWNfZmFjdHMiOiBbCiAgICB7InN1YmplY3QiOiAiU3RhcnNtaXRoIiwgInJlbGF0aW9uIjogInJlbWl4ZWQiLAogICAgICJvYmplY3QiOiAiQmFkIFJvbWFuY2UiLAogICAgICJ0aXRsZSI6ICJTdGFyc21pdGgiLCAic2VudGVuY2VfaWQiOiA0fSwKICAgIHsic3ViamVjdCI6ICJUaGUgUmVtaXggKExhZHkgR2FnYSBhbGJ1bSkiLCAicmVsYXRpb24iOiAiY29udGFpbnMgcmVtaXhlcyBvZiBzb25ncyBmcm9tIiwKICAgICAib2JqZWN0IjogIlRoZSBGYW1lIiwKICAgICAidGl0bGUiOiAiVGhlIFJlbWl4IChMYWR5IEdhZ2EgYWxidW0pIiwgInNlbnRlbmNlX2lkIjogMX0KICBdLAogICJyZWFzb25pbmciOiB7Im9wZXJhdGlvbiI6ICJicmlkZ2UiLCAiZmFjdF9pbmRpY2VzIjogWzAsIDFdLAogICAgICAgICAgICAgICAgInJlc3VsdCI6ICJCYWQgUm9tYW5jZSJ9Cn0=)

{

"final_answer": "Bad Romance",

"supporting_facts": \[

{"title": "Starsmith", "sentence_id": 4},

{"title": "The Remix (Lady Gaga album)", "sentence_id": 1}

\],

"atomic_facts": \[

{"subject": "Starsmith", "relation": "remixed",

"object": "Bad Romance",

"title": "Starsmith", "sentence_id": 4},

{"subject": "The Remix (Lady Gaga album)", "relation": "contains remixes
of songs from",

"object": "The Fame",

"title": "The Remix (Lady Gaga album)", "sentence_id": 1}

\],

"reasoning": {"operation": "bridge", "fact_indices": \[0, 1\],

"result": "Bad Romance"}

}

The candidate now returns Bad Romance with both correct supporting
facts, raising this item’s Joint F1 from 0 to 1. The candidate increases
the mean Joint F1 on the validation set and Greedy accepts it. This
validation gain includes direct reuse of feedback on repeated validation
questions.

We now turn to the test set and consider the following example.

[⬇](data:text/plain;base64,MjAxNCBTL1MgaXMgdGhlIGRlYnV0IGFsYnVtIG9mIGEgU291dGggS29yZWFuIGJveSBncm91cCB0aGF0IHdhcyBmb3JtZWQgYnkgd2hvPw==)

2014 S/S is the debut album of a South Korean boy group that was formed
by who?

The correct answer is YG Entertainment. The question asks who formed the
South Korean boy group whose debut album is 2014 S/S. Answering it
requires connecting two pieces of information. First, the paragraph from
2014 S/S identifies WINNER as the group that released the album. Second,
the paragraph from Winner (band) states that WINNER was formed by YG
Entertainment. Together, these facts connect the album to the group and
then to the organization that formed it. Before the update, the
incumbent selects eight of the ten available paragraphs, including both
of these sources.

[⬇](data:text/plain;base64,ewogICJmaW5hbF9hbnN3ZXIiOiAiWUcgRW50ZXJ0YWlubWVudCIsCiAgInN1cHBvcnRpbmdfZmFjdHMiOiBbCiAgICB7InRpdGxlIjogIjIwMTQgUy9TIiwgInNlbnRlbmNlX2lkIjogMH0sCiAgICB7InRpdGxlIjogIldpbm5lciAoYmFuZCkiLCAic2VudGVuY2VfaWQiOiAwfQogIF0sCiAgImF0b21pY19mYWN0cyI6IFsKICAgIHsic3ViamVjdCI6ICIyMDE0IFMvUyIsICJyZWxhdGlvbiI6ICJpcyBkZWJ1dCBhbGJ1bSBvZiIsCiAgICAgIm9iamVjdCI6ICJXSU5ORVIiLAogICAgICJ0aXRsZSI6ICIyMDE0IFMvUyIsICJzZW50ZW5jZV9pZCI6IDB9LAogICAgeyJzdWJqZWN0IjogIldJTk5FUiIsICJyZWxhdGlvbiI6ICJmb3JtZWQgYnkiLAogICAgICJvYmplY3QiOiAiWUcgRW50ZXJ0YWlubWVudCIsCiAgICAgInRpdGxlIjogIldpbm5lciAoYmFuZCkiLCAic2VudGVuY2VfaWQiOiAwfQogIF0sCiAgInJlYXNvbmluZyI6IHsib3BlcmF0aW9uIjogImJyaWRnZSIsICJmYWN0X2luZGljZXMiOiBbMCwgMV0sCiAgICAgICAgICAgICAgICAicmVzdWx0IjogIllHIEVudGVydGFpbm1lbnQifQp9)

{

"final_answer": "YG Entertainment",

"supporting_facts": \[

{"title": "2014 S/S", "sentence_id": 0},

{"title": "Winner (band)", "sentence_id": 0}

\],

"atomic_facts": \[

{"subject": "2014 S/S", "relation": "is debut album of",

"object": "WINNER",

"title": "2014 S/S", "sentence_id": 0},

{"subject": "WINNER", "relation": "formed by",

"object": "YG Entertainment",

"title": "Winner (band)", "sentence_id": 0}

\],

"reasoning": {"operation": "bridge", "fact_indices": \[0, 1\],

"result": "YG Entertainment"}

}

Both steps cite the correct sources: sentence 0 of 2014 S/S identifies
the group, and sentence 0 of Winner (band) identifies its founder. Both
the answer and the supporting facts are correct, resulting in a Joint F1
score of $`1`$.

This question retrieves the Lady Gaga memory through the same keyword
album. After retrieving this memory, the system changes how it selects
evidence paragraphs and limits the number retained in the context. Under
this limit, the selected context omits Winner (band), which contains the
evidence needed to identify who formed WINNER.

[⬇](data:text/plain;base64,ewogICJmaW5hbF9hbnN3ZXIiOiAiWUcgRW50ZXJ0YWlubWVudCIsCiAgInN1cHBvcnRpbmdfZmFjdHMiOiBbCiAgICB7InRpdGxlIjogIjIwMTQgUy9TIiwgInNlbnRlbmNlX2lkIjogMX0KICBdLAogICJhdG9taWNfZmFjdHMiOiBbCiAgICB7InN1YmplY3QiOiAiMjAxNCBTL1MiLCAicmVsYXRpb24iOiAiaXMgZGVidXQgYWxidW0gb2YiLAogICAgICJvYmplY3QiOiAiV0lOTkVSIiwKICAgICAidGl0bGUiOiAiMjAxNCBTL1MiLCAic2VudGVuY2VfaWQiOiAwfSwKICAgIHsic3ViamVjdCI6ICJXSU5ORVIiLCAicmVsYXRpb24iOiAiZm9ybWVkIGJ5IiwKICAgICAib2JqZWN0IjogIllHIEVudGVydGFpbm1lbnQiLAogICAgICJ0aXRsZSI6ICIyMDE0IFMvUyIsICJzZW50ZW5jZV9pZCI6IDF9CiAgXSwKICAicmVhc29uaW5nIjogeyJvcGVyYXRpb24iOiAiYnJpZGdlIiwgImZhY3RfaW5kaWNlcyI6IFswLCAxXSwKICAgICAgICAgICAgICAgICJyZXN1bHQiOiAiWUcgRW50ZXJ0YWlubWVudCJ9Cn0=)

{

"final_answer": "YG Entertainment",

"supporting_facts": \[

{"title": "2014 S/S", "sentence_id": 1}

\],

"atomic_facts": \[

{"subject": "2014 S/S", "relation": "is debut album of",

"object": "WINNER",

"title": "2014 S/S", "sentence_id": 0},

{"subject": "WINNER", "relation": "formed by",

"object": "YG Entertainment",

"title": "2014 S/S", "sentence_id": 1}

\],

"reasoning": {"operation": "bridge", "fact_indices": \[0, 1\],

"result": "YG Entertainment"}

}

The answer remains YG Entertainment, but the response now attributes the
formed by relation to sentence 1 of 2014 S/S. Its submitted supporting
fact list contains only that sentence and omits both gold supporting
facts.

[⬇](data:text/plain;base64,SXQgd2FzIHJlbGVhc2VkIG9uIEF1Z3VzdCAxMiwgMjAxNCBieSB0aGUgZ3JvdXAncyByZWNvcmQgbGFiZWwsIFlHIEVudGVydGFpbm1lbnQu)

It was released on August 12, 2014 by the group’s record label, YG
Entertainment.

This sentence concerns the album’s release by a record label. It does
not establish who formed the group. The answer happens to remain
correct, so Answer F1 remains 1, while supporting fact F1 and Joint F1
fall to 0. On the full test set, the accepted update lowers the mean
Joint F1 from 54.55% to 48.00%: 13 items decline and none improve. This
example shows how a memory can repair a repeated validation question
while causing evidence selection to lose a required source on a new
question.
